Pantanal seasonal group collections

🗓️ Seasons · 📋 Collections · 🌎 Group rankings · Individual audit

Collections combine spatially bounded groups to maximize the number of represented jaguars within each seasonal sampling period. Groups retain their quality and spatial rankings. Membership counts describe selected evidence; informative plain-MaxEnt route counts are determined by export diagnostics.

Selection, seasons and interpretation

Observed fixes are selected near a two-hour interval, with a 1.5–2.5-hour regular band. Gaps greater than four hours split trajectories. Retained endpoints of tolerated longer intervals share a period-wide 20% budget; subtrajectories contain at least five selected observations, and short-fragment loss is limited to 5% during selection. Main windows meet the configured schedule-density and observed-span thresholds. Measured temporal or spatial relaxations remain flagged.

Season intervals use exact date boundaries; eligible windows may use adjoining buffer dates. Red bold italic month names indicate that some included observations fall outside the season core; other observations in the same month may fall within it. Pink chart columns and repeated-ID labels identify animals belonging to several groups. An extra membership is each occurrence after an animal’s first group membership.

Source timestamps identify actual observation coverage. A shaded first-to-last span does not imply uninterrupted telemetry. MCP95 polygons use full-study observations; collection envelopes outline member polygons and are spatial summaries. Export ownership records identify which group/window evidence is included when memberships overlap. All versions of one animal must retain the same training/validation/test assignment.

🌎 Group rankings

Reading the ranks: group ranks are unique across the report. Main groups precede relaxed groups; within each category, ranking prioritizes total retained fixes multiplied by Jain’s index, followed by schedule density, irregular-endpoint fraction and map area. Collection ranks restart within each season and prioritize unique-animal coverage, fewer repeated memberships, fewer groups and constituent group ranks. Every retained collection covers the generated eligible animal union; the bounded greedy search does not establish a minimum-group optimum.

Optional weekly density view · retained selector counts

Only canonically selected collection memberships contribute. Repeated source events are counted once per animal. Cell numbers and green shading encode the retained fix count for one individual in one calendar week: lighter green means fewer fixes and darker green means more. The labeled color bar spans zero to the largest count in this collection, with the same scale across all its season-year panels. Different collections can have different maxima; counts may exceed the 85-fix seven-day reference under the interval tolerance. Dashed cells indicate less than 168 hours of selected-window envelope within the calendar week. This envelope is not continuous telemetry coverage. Red underlines indicate buffer-only observations; half-width red underlines indicate mixed core/buffer observations. Blank cells mean no retained selected observations.

Optional weekly density view · retained selector counts

Only canonically selected collection memberships contribute. Repeated source events are counted once per animal. Why point CV and Jain's index?

Here nᵢ is the retained fix count for jaguar i, and k is the number of included individuals. These metrics describe how evenly the selected observations are distributed across animals. Counts have a meaningful zero and a positive mean, so relative dispersion is interpretable. Equal contributions are an explicit cohort-design preference; actual training influence also depends on the model's loss aggregation and weighting.

Point CV: CV = σ / μ, where μ = Σnᵢ / k and σ = √[Σ(nᵢ − μ)² / k]. We use the population divisor k because we describe the complete selected group, rather than estimate variation in a larger population. CV = 0 means equal counts; CV = 0.04 means the standard deviation is 4% of the mean. Lower is more balanced. The NIST definition distinguishes this population CV from the sample version using k − 1.

Jain's index: J = (Σnᵢ)² / [k Σnᵢ²]. It equals 1 for equal counts and decreases with imbalance. Multiplying every animal's count by the same factor leaves both CV and Jain unchanged, making them useful complements to total fixes. The original Jain, Chiu & Hawe (1984) measure concerns resource-allocation fairness; using it for jaguar observation counts is our adaptation.

Related count-based case studies: Hönig, Zhao & Mullins (2022), DAdaQuant, §4.2, use CV of client dataset sample counts to characterize quantity imbalance in federated learning. Their application concerns communication compression, rather than choosing equal dataset sizes. Jeong & Kim (2022), CATA-Fed, §5.3.2 and Fig. 13a, use Jain's index on client-selection counts to assess equality of training opportunities. These establish relevant uses of count-balance measures; neither study validates a jaguar sampling threshold or an improvement in IRL prediction.

Two views of the same property: for these counts and the population standard deviation, J = 1 / (1 + CV²), exactly. Chen & Hooker (2023), §4.1, also give this relationship. The two values are mathematically redundant. Only Jain enters the ranking score, total fixes × J = total fixes / (1 + CV²); CV is an interpretation aid, not a second penalty. Both are shown alongside the individual bars and histogram so the underlying counts remain inspectable.

Interpretation: balanced counts do not establish independent fixes, comparable habitat coverage, effective sample size, or predictive accuracy. Equally small and equally large datasets both have CV = 0 and J = 1; total fixes and animal counts must be considered alongside balance. The minimum week-coverage screen already constrains count variation, so Jain values near 1 are expected. Validate model performance with animal-level train/test grouping.
